I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Dr. [Name]. Um, thank you for seeing me. So, I guess... well, I work in television and film production, mostly as a camera operator and a video editor. I've been doing it for years, ever since I got my bachelor's, and it's a really fast-paced environment, which I usually love, but lately... well, what finally scared me into actually making this appointment and coming in today is that I just keep making these really, really dumb technical mistakes. \n\nAnd I mean mistakes I absolutely know better than to make. It's not a lack of experience, it's just... I don't know, my brain just skips over things. Like, I'll be offloading footage after a twelve-hour shoot and I'll label the camera cards completely wrong, which is a massive headache for the whole post-production team. Or I'll be sitting in the edit bay and I'll just completely miss a specific timecode note the director gave me. \n\nBut the worst part, the part that really freaked me out and made me call your office, was when I exported a final client cut recently, and it had the completely wrong lower-third graphic on the screen with the CEO's name misspelled. And the crazy thing is, I checked it! I literally sat there, watched the timeline, told myself I was checking it for errors, and I still somehow just sent it off wrong. It's so incredibly embarrassing, and in this industry, your reputation is everything. If you're the editor who ruins a deadline because of a sloppy export, people stop hiring you. I just don't understand how I can look right at something, know exactly how it's supposed to be done, and still mess up the basic details like that. It's just making me so anxious about going into work every day.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks or months, have you noticed changes in your mood — like feeling persistently sad, empty, or down — separate from just the frustration about these work mistakes?\n\nPatient: No, no, not sad. I mean, not like that, anyway. I definitely get upset, and I might even cry out of pure frustration when I get home, but it's not like a dark cloud of sadness or feeling empty, you know? It's really just the anxiety and the stress from the mistakes. Like, just last Tuesday, I was driving home from the studio, and it was raining, and I just gripped the steering wheel so hard my knuckles were white, just replaying that lower-third graphic mistake over and over in my head. But that's not depression, I don't think. That's just me being incredibly mad at myself because I know I'm better than that. \n\nBecause when I'm not working, or when things actually go right—which they do, sometimes I nail a really complex multi-cam edit and it feels amazing—I feel totally fine! I have plenty of energy, I want to see my friends, I still love going out to the movies, which is huge for me since I work in film, you know? Some people get burned out and can't even look at a screen after a long week, but I still love it. I'm still me. \n\nIt's literally just this... this wall I hit when I have to focus on the tedious technical details, and then I mess them up, and *then* I feel bad. But it's a very specific \"I'm going to lose my job and ruin my reputation\" kind of bad, not a... not a sitting in the dark feeling empty kind of bad. Does that make sense? I just don't want you to think I'm depressed, because I really don't feel depressed, just... on edge. Constantly on edge about what stupid little detail I'm going to screw up next, and how much it's going to cost the production.\n\nDoctor: When you're not at work or thinking about work, do you notice that you have trouble concentrating or remembering things in your everyday life — like forgetting conversations, losing your keys, or having a hard time following a TV show or book — or is it mainly just at work when you're stressed?\n\nPatient: Well, um, it's funny you ask that, because I don't really think about it that much outside of work, but... no, I guess I don't really have trouble with that stuff? Like, if I'm watching a movie or a TV show, I'm completely locked in. I mean, obviously, I'm looking at the lighting and the cuts because of what I do for a living, but I never lose the plot or forget what's happening. I can sit through a three-hour director's cut of something and be totally absorbed the entire time. \n\nAnd as for conversations... no, I don't think I forget them. I'm actually the one who usually remembers random things my friends told me months ago. Like, my friend Sarah was talking about this very specific brand of vintage camera lens she wanted back in, I don't know, maybe October? And I remembered it just the other day when I saw one online and sent her the link. So my memory for things like that is fine. \n\nAnd losing things... I mean, everyone misplaces their keys once in a while, right? If I'm rushing out the door because I'm running late for a shoot, maybe I have to pat down my pockets or check the kitchen counter, but it's not like a constant, everyday struggle where I'm tearing the house apart looking for my wallet. My apartment is actually pretty organized, mostly because if it wasn't, I'd go crazy. I have specific hooks by the door for my keys and my work badge. So no, I wouldn't say I have a hard time with any of that in my normal life. It really just feels like it's the work stuff, the pressure and the technical details, that completely short-circuits my brain.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on routine, non-deadline tasks — like organizing files, doing basic maintenance on your equipment, or even just reviewing footage that doesn't have time pressure attached to it — do you find yourself making careless mistakes then too, or does it really only happen when you're under pressure or rushing?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that’s the thing. That is exactly what I was trying to explain earlier when I was talking about what finally scared me into coming in here. Because if it was just happening when we were on a tight turnaround, like a crazy same-day edit where the producers are literally standing over my shoulder, I would totally just chalk it up to stress. Everyone makes mistakes when they are rushing, right? \n\nBut... no. It happens even when I have all the time in the world. It happens during the most mundane, routine stuff. I can be just sitting in the ingest bay with my coffee on a quiet morning, nobody is breathing down my neck, the deadline isn't for another week. I'm just offloading footage, which should be the easiest thing in the world, and like I was saying before, I will still somehow label those camera cards completely wrong. \n\nAnd it’s the same with reviewing footage or organizing my project bins. I can be reviewing an interview without any time pressure at all, just casually watching it down to pull the best takes, and I will still completely miss a specific timecode note the director gave me. It's like my eyes are open, I'm looking right at the screen, but my brain just decides not to process the numbers. \n\nEven that final client cut I told you about, the one with the wrong lower-third graphic and the misspelled CEO name? I actually had a full forty-eight hours to deliver that. I wasn't rushing at all! I literally sat there at my desk, leaned back in my chair, and told myself that I was going to do a slow, careful check for errors. I watched the whole timeline. I checked it, I really did, and I still exported it wrong and sent it off. \n\nSo, yeah, it's definitely not just the pressure or the rushing. The pressure makes the consequences worse, obviously, but the actual mistakes, those really dumb technical mistakes that I absolutely know better than to make, they happen even when I'm just doing basic file management or routine work. And honestly, I think that's what's so terrifying about it to me. If I can't even get the basic, low-stakes stuff right when I'm totally relaxed and have plenty of time, how am I supposed to trust myself at all? It just makes me feel like I'm losing my edge completely.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these careless mistakes and trouble with focus on technical details? Was this something that's been happening your whole life, or is this a relatively new development for you in the past few months or years?\n\nPatient: My whole life? Oh, gosh, no. No, definitely not my whole life. I mean, I’ve always been a very high-energy person, you know, always moving a million miles a minute, which is actually great when you're on a busy set, but the actual mistakes? The dropping the ball on the technical details and just totally missing things right in front of my face? That is definitely newer. \n\nIf I really sit down and trace it back to when it started getting bad... like, really disruptive to my job... it’s been going on for about two years and two months. Yeah, exactly two years and two months, because it was right at the start of the year, two years ago, when our studio went through this massive company readjustment. \n\nSee, before that readjustment, things were structured a lot differently. I had a totally different rhythm. But then management came in, they restructured the departments to cut costs, and they completely changed how my shoots and edit reviews run. Like, completely overhauled the entire pipeline. It used to be that we had a very collaborative, in-person review process—we'd all sit in a bay, the producers, the director, and we'd watch the cut together and talk through it. It was very engaging, very dynamic. I thrived in that environment. \n\nBut the readjustment changed all of that. Now, the shoots are scheduled differently, so I'm bouncing between projects way more without a lot of transition time, and the edit reviews are almost entirely remote and asynchronous. I have to upload my cuts to this web portal, fill out all these tedious metadata fields, type up my own technical logs, and then just wait for timecode notes to be emailed back to me in this massive spreadsheet. \n\nIt completely shifted the burden of all that tiny, granular technical stuff onto my plate in a way it wasn't before, and stripped away the parts of the process that kept me engaged. And that is exactly when this started. Two years and two months of just... constantly missing things in the new system. I kept telling myself, \"Oh, you're just adjusting to the new workflow, it'll click eventually, everyone takes time to learn a new system.\" But it hasn't clicked! It's been over two years, and I'm still making the same stupid mistakes on the reviews that I was making the week they rolled the changes out. So, yeah, it's not a lifelong thing at all, it's very specifically tied to when they changed how we work. I just... I don't know why I can't adapt to it like everyone else seems to have.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you've always been a high-energy person, moving a mile a minute. Before the company restructuring two years ago, did you ever struggle with staying organized, keeping track of details, or managing multiple tasks at once — even if it wasn't as noticeable in your old work environment?\n\nPatient: Honestly? No. No, not at all. I mean, if anything, I was actually known for being the one who could juggle a million things at once. Because when you're a camera operator and an editor—which I do both, you know, depending on the project, and that’s pretty common in the commercial space—you absolutely have to be organized. And I was! Before they changed everything, I was the person everyone relied on to keep all the plates spinning. \n\nI could be on a busy set, managing three different camera setups, keeping track of exactly which lenses we used for which specific shots so the continuity matched perfectly in post, and I’d be talking to the director about the next setup while simultaneously packing away the fragile gear from the last one. Multitasking was just... it was my bread and butter. It never felt like a struggle. Even going way back to when I was getting my bachelor's degree in film, I was always the one producing the big senior thesis projects because I could handle having my hands in everything. The details never slipped through the cracks back then because the environment was just so dynamic and alive. \n\nAnd it was the exact same in the edit bay. I had my project bins organized flawlessly—audio here, b-roll there, interviews color-coded by subject. And it wasn't something I had to force myself to do or white-knuckle my way through, it was just how I worked. The fast pace actually made me more organized, if that makes sense? Like, the momentum of moving a mile a minute kept me sharp. I thrived on that kind of controlled chaos, honestly. \n\nSo, no, I didn't struggle with keeping track of details or managing multiple tasks back then, not even subtly. It never felt like a heavy lift, and I never dropped the ball on the important stuff. I had my own rhythm, my own system, and it worked perfectly for me for years and years. I mean, I wouldn't have gotten to the level I'm at in my career if I couldn't handle the details, right? You just can't survive in television or film if you're inherently disorganized. I was doing great. It really is just this new, slow, isolated, spreadsheet-heavy workflow that completely paralyzes me. But before that? I was completely on top of it.\n\nDoctor: When you were in school — elementary, middle, high school, college — did teachers or professors ever give you feedback that you were careless, didn't pay attention to detail, or made silly mistakes on assignments or tests, even back then?\n\nPatient: Careless mistakes? No, see, that's the thing. Like I was saying about my film projects and how I've always been able to juggle a million things, the actual work was never the problem back then. I wasn't dropping the ball on the assignments or making silly errors on my tests. \n\nBut... if you're asking if teachers had feedback for me, oh, absolutely. They had plenty of feedback, it just wasn't about my attention to detail. It was... well, it was about my energy. Even way back in elementary school, I was just constantly getting talked to. I remember my teachers actually had to move my desk all the time. It was like this whole regular production where they'd move me right up next to the chalkboard, or isolate me in the back corner by the reading rug, because I was literally always out of my seat. I just couldn't sit still in those little plastic chairs, it felt like physical torture. \n\nI'd be up wandering around, or I'd just be blurting answers out loud before the teacher even finished asking the question. I just knew the answer and it would fly out of my mouth. And I was always bothering my classmates, like leaning over to talk to them or just being in their space, and the crazy thing is, I wouldn't even realize I was doing it until the teacher snapped at me to sit down and be quiet. It was just this physical need to move and engage with whatever was happening around me. \n\nSo my report cards were always full of comments about how I needed to practice self-control, or how I needed to learn to stay in my designated area, but never about my actual schoolwork being sloppy. I did fine on the tests! I just... I probably took them while standing up or bouncing my leg so hard the whole table shook. By the time I got to college, it was actually a lot better because I could just walk across campus between classes or throw myself into producing a massive, hands-on student film where I was running around set all day. The energy was an asset then. But sitting at a desk in grade school? Total disaster. But yeah, the careless mistakes thing? The missing the details? No, that really is just a completely recent thing tied to this new, agonizingly slow editing workflow I'm stuck in.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing those detailed technical tasks now — like labeling camera cards or reviewing timecodes — do you notice that you're fidgeting, feeling restless, or having trouble sitting still the way you used to in school? Or is it more that your mind just seems to wander or skip over the information even though your body feels calm?\n\nPatient: Calm? Oh, gosh, no. No, my body is definitely not calm. If anything, it's... it's almost exactly like it was in school, just, you know, manifested differently because I'm an adult in an office and not a kid on a reading rug. \n\nWhen I'm stuck doing those technical logs or staring at that massive spreadsheet trying to match up the timecodes, I am just constantly in motion. It's like this physical pressure builds up the longer I have to sit still and look at the tiny text. Like, especially in the edit reviews now, I’m constantly clicking a pen. Just over and over. I actually broke the spring in my favorite ballpoint last week because I was doing it so aggressively while trying to force myself to focus on this one incredibly tedious metadata field. \n\nAnd if I'm not clicking a pen, my knee is just going a mile a minute. I'm always bouncing my knee under the table, sometimes so hard I'm honestly surprised I haven't bruised my kneecap on the underside of the desk. And we have these fancy rolling chairs in the bays, which are supposed to be ergonomic, but for me, they're just... I don't know, an outlet for the restlessness? I am constantly rolling my chair back and forth, pushing off the edge of the desk, rolling back in, swiveling side to side while I wait for a file to upload or while I'm trying to read a note. \n\nIt’s actually become this whole running thing at the studio. People have started joking that they can hear me before they see me. They’ll be walking down the hall and hear the *click-click-click* of the pen and the squeak of the chair wheels and they know exactly where I am and what I'm doing. Because I literally cannot just sit static and look at the screen. The slower and more isolating the task is, the more my body just completely rebels and tries to create its own momentum. So, yeah, the physical restlessness is absolutely still there, it hasn't gone away at all, it's just channeled into office supplies and furniture now instead of wandering around a classroom.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on something that's genuinely engaging and interesting to you — like a complex multi-cam edit you mentioned earlier, or maybe color grading a really visually stunning sequence — do you find yourself making those same careless technical mistakes, or does your focus stay sharp on the details when the work itself is more stimulating?\n\nPatient: Right? See, that's exactly the distinction, and it's so unbelievably frustrating. Because if I'm doing something I actually care about—like, say I'm color grading a really gorgeous, moody sequence, which I love doing, right? I'm adjusting the shadows, pushing the midtones, making sure the skin tones match perfectly across three different camera sensors—my focus is absolute. I am completely locked in. I don't miss a single pixel. Or if I'm doing a fast-paced multi-cam edit where I'm cutting right on the beat of the music, I'm incredibly sharp. The details don't slip at all. \n\nBut... and this is really the whole reason I'm here, honestly, what finally scared me into coming in to see a doctor... is that with all this new, tedious workflow stuff, I keep making these incredibly dumb technical mistakes that I absolutely know better than to make. It's like my brain just completely shuts off if the task isn't stimulating enough. \n\nLike, I'll be labeling camera cards, which is the most basic, Day-One intern level stuff in the world. You just put the date, the camera letter, the project code. And I'll label them wrong! I'll just put the wrong date, or I'll swap A-cam and B-cam, which causes a huge headache for the assistant editors. Or I'll be going through the producer's spreadsheet and I will completely miss a timecode note. Just skip right over it like it wasn't even written on the page, even though I'm staring right at it. \n\nAnd the absolute worst one—the one that really made me pick up the phone and make this appointment—was a couple of weeks ago. I exported a final client cut, like the absolute final version meant for the client to approve for broadcast, and it had the wrong lower-third graphic on it. A lower-third, if you don't know, is that text at the bottom of the screen with the interview subject's name and job title. I had the completely wrong person's title up there. \n\nAnd the truly terrifying part? I checked it! I swear to you, I watched the export down before I sent the link. I looked right at the monitor, I checked the graphics, and I still somehow didn't see it. It's like my eyes saw the words but my brain just didn't register that they were the wrong words because I was so under-stimulated by the process of doing quality control on a static file. The client had to flag it, and I was just... I was mortified. I mean, my reputation is everything in this industry, you know? You live and die by your reliability. And I'm out here making these embarrassing rookie mistakes on the boring stuff, even when I'm trying so, so hard to double-check my work. So, yeah, if the work is stimulating, I'm golden. If it's not... I'm suddenly dropping the ball on things I've known how to do for fifteen years.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. No, that really is just a completely recent thing tied to this new, agonizingly slow editing workflow I'm stuck in.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing those detailed technical tasks now — like labeling camera cards or reviewing timecodes — do you notice that you're fidgeting, feeling restless, or having trouble sitting still the way you used to in school? Or is it more that your mind just seems to wander or skip over the information even though your body feels calm?\n\nPatient: Calm? Oh, gosh, no. No, my body is definitely not calm. If anything, it's... it's almost exactly like it was in school, just, you know, manifested differently because I'm an adult in an office and not a kid on a reading rug. \n\nWhen I'm stuck doing those technical logs or staring at that massive spreadsheet trying to match up the timecodes, I am just constantly in motion. It's like this physical pressure builds up the longer I have to sit still and look at the tiny text. Like, especially in the edit reviews now, I’m constantly clicking a pen. Just over and over. I actually broke the spring in my favorite ballpoint last week because I was doing it so aggressively while trying to force myself to focus on this one incredibly tedious metadata field. \n\nAnd if I'm not clicking a pen, my knee is just going a mile a minute. I'm always bouncing my knee under the table, sometimes so hard I'm honestly surprised I haven't bruised my kneecap on the underside of the desk. And we have these fancy rolling chairs in the bays, which are supposed to be ergonomic, but for me, they're just... I don't know, an outlet for the restlessness? I am constantly rolling my chair back and forth, pushing off the edge of the desk, rolling back in, swiveling side to side while I wait for a file to upload or while I'm trying to read a note. \n\nIt’s actually become this whole running thing at the studio. People have started joking that they can hear me before they see me. They’ll be walking down the hall and hear the *click-click-click* of the pen and the squeak of the chair wheels and they know exactly where I am and what I'm doing. Because I literally cannot just sit static and look at the screen. The slower and more isolating the task is, the more my body just completely rebels and tries to create its own momentum. So, yeah, the physical restlessness is absolutely still there, it hasn't gone away at all, it's just channeled into office supplies and furniture now instead of wandering around a classroom.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on something that's genuinely engaging and interesting to you — like a complex multi-cam edit you mentioned earlier, or maybe color grading a really visually stunning sequence — do you find yourself making those same careless technical mistakes, or does your focus stay sharp on the details when the work itself is more stimulating?\n\nPatient: Right? See, that's exactly the distinction, and it's so unbelievably frustrating. Because if I'm doing something I actually care about—like, say I'm color grading a really gorgeous, moody sequence, which I love doing, right? I'm adjusting the shadows, pushing the midtones, making sure the skin tones match perfectly across three different camera sensors—my focus is absolute. I am completely locked in. I don't miss a single pixel. Or if I'm doing a fast-paced multi-cam edit where I'm cutting right on the beat of the music, I'm incredibly sharp. The details don't slip at all. \n\nBut... and this is really the whole reason I'm here, honestly, what finally scared me into coming in to see a doctor... is that with all this new, tedious workflow stuff, I keep making these incredibly dumb technical mistakes that I absolutely know better than to make. It's like my brain just completely shuts off if the task isn't stimulating enough. \n\nLike, I'll be labeling camera cards, which is the most basic, Day-One intern level stuff in the world. You just put the date, the camera letter, the project code. And I'll label them wrong! I'll just put the wrong date, or I'll swap A-cam and B-cam, which causes a huge headache for the assistant editors. Or I'll be going through the producer's spreadsheet and I will completely miss a timecode note. Just skip right over it like it wasn't even written on the page, even though I'm staring right at it. \n\nAnd the absolute worst one—the one that really made me pick up the phone and make this appointment—was a couple of weeks ago. I exported a final client cut, like the absolute final version meant for the client to approve for broadcast, and it had the wrong lower-third graphic on it. A lower-third, if you don't know, is that text at the bottom of the screen with the interview subject's name and job title. I had the completely wrong person's title up there. \n\nAnd the truly terrifying part? I checked it! I swear to you, I watched the export down before I sent the link. I looked right at the monitor, I checked the graphics, and I still somehow didn't see it. It's like my eyes saw the words but my brain just didn't register that they were the wrong words because I was so under-stimulated by the process of doing quality control on a static file. The client had to flag it, and I was just... I was mortified. I mean, my reputation is everything in this industry, you know? You live and die by your reliability. And I'm out here making these embarrassing rookie mistakes on the boring stuff, even when I'm trying so, so hard to double-check my work. So, yeah, if the work is stimulating, I'm golden. If it's not... I'm suddenly dropping the ball on things I've known how to do for fifteen years.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I feel like I'm having a bit of déjà vu here, did I... I mean, I think I actually just went on a whole tangent about this exact thing, didn't I? I can be just sitting in the ingest bay with my coffee on a quiet morning, nobody is breathing down my neck, the deadline isn't for another week. I'm just offloading footage, which should be the easiest thing in the world, and like I was saying before, I will still somehow label those camera cards completely wrong. \n\nAnd it’s the same with reviewing footage or organizing my project bins. I can be reviewing an interview without any time pressure at all, just casually watching it down to pull the best takes, and I will still completely miss a specific timecode note the director gave me. It's like my eyes are open, I'm looking right at the screen, but my brain just decides not to process the numbers. \n\nEven that final client cut I told you about, the one with the wrong lower-third graphic and the misspelled CEO name? I actually had a full forty-eight hours to deliver that. I wasn't rushing at all! I literally sat there at my desk, leaned back in my chair, and told myself that I was going to do a slow, careful check for errors. I watched the whole timeline. I checked it, I really did, and I still exported it wrong and sent it off. \n\nSo, yeah, it's definitely not just the pressure or the rushing. The pressure makes the consequences worse, obviously, but the actual mistakes, those really dumb technical mistakes that I absolutely know better than to make, they happen even when I'm just doing basic file management or routine work. And honestly, I think that's what's so terrifying about it to me. If I can't even get the basic, low-stakes stuff right when I'm totally relaxed and have plenty of time, how am I supposed to trust myself at all? It just makes me feel like I'm losing my edge completely.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these careless mistakes and trouble with focus on technical details? Was this something that's been happening your whole life, or is this a relatively new development for you in the past few months or years?\n\nPatient: My whole life? Oh, gosh, no. No, definitely not my whole life. I mean, I’ve always been a very high-energy person, you know, always moving a million miles a minute, which is actually great when you're on a busy set, but the actual mistakes? The dropping the ball on the technical details and just totally missing things right in front of my face? That is definitely newer. \n\nIf I really sit down and trace it back to when it started getting bad... like, really disruptive to my job... it’s been going on for about two years and two months. Yeah, exactly two years and two months, because it was right at the start of the year, two years ago, when our studio went through this massive company readjustment. \n\nSee, before that readjustment, things were structured a lot differently. I had a totally different rhythm. But then management came in, they restructured the departments to cut costs, and they completely changed how my shoots and edit reviews run. Like, completely overhauled the entire pipeline. It used to be that we had a very collaborative, in-person review process—we'd all sit in a bay, the producers, the director, and we'd watch the cut together and talk through it. It was very engaging, very dynamic. I thrived in that environment. \n\nBut the readjustment changed all of that. Now, the shoots are scheduled differently, so I'm bouncing between projects way more without a lot of transition time, and the edit reviews are almost entirely remote and asynchronous. I have to upload my cuts to this web portal, fill out all these tedious metadata fields, type up my own technical logs, and then just wait for timecode notes to be emailed back to me in this massive spreadsheet. \n\nIt completely shifted the burden of all that tiny, granular technical stuff onto my plate in a way it wasn't before, and stripped away the parts of the process that kept me engaged. And that is exactly when this started. Two years and two months of just... constantly missing things in the new system. I kept telling myself, \"Oh, you're just adjusting to the new workflow, it'll click eventually, everyone takes time to learn a new system.\" But it hasn't clicked! It's been over two years, and I'm still making the same stupid mistakes on the reviews that I was making the week they rolled the changes out. So, yeah, it's not a lifelong thing at all, it's very specifically tied to when they changed how we work. I just... I don't know why I can't adapt to it like everyone else seems to have.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you've always been a high-energy person, moving a mile a minute. Before the company restructuring two years ago, did you ever struggle with staying organized, keeping track of details, or managing multiple tasks at once — even if it wasn't as noticeable in your old work environment?\n\nPatient: Honestly? No. No, not at all. I mean, if anything, I was actually known for being the one who could juggle a million things at once. Because when you're a camera operator and an editor—which I do both, you know, depending on the project, and that’s pretty common in the commercial space—you absolutely have to be organized. And I was! Before they changed everything, I was the person everyone relied on to keep all the plates spinning. \n\nI could be on a busy set, managing three different camera setups, keeping track of exactly which lenses we used for which specific shots so the continuity matched perfectly in post, and I’d be talking to the director about the next setup while simultaneously packing away the fragile gear from the last one. Multitasking was just... it was my bread and butter. It never felt like a struggle. Even going way back to when I was getting my bachelor's degree in film, I was always the one producing the big senior thesis projects because I could handle having my hands in everything. The details never slipped through the cracks back then because the environment was just so dynamic and alive. \n\nAnd it was the exact same in the edit bay. I had my project bins organized flawlessly—audio here, b-roll there, interviews color-coded by subject. And it wasn't something I had to force myself to do or white-knuckle my way through, it was just how I worked. The fast pace actually made me more organized, if that makes sense? Like, the momentum of moving a mile a minute kept me sharp. I thrived on that kind of controlled chaos, honestly. \n\nSo, no, I didn't struggle with keeping track of details or managing multiple tasks back then, not even subtly. It never felt like a heavy lift, and I never dropped the ball on the important stuff. I had my own rhythm, my own system, and it worked perfectly for me for years and years. I mean, I wouldn't have gotten to the level I'm at in my career if I couldn't handle the details, right? You just can't survive in television or film if you're inherently disorganized. I was doing great. It really is just this new, slow, isolated, spreadsheet-heavy workflow that completely paralyzes me. But before that? I was completely on top of it.\n\nDoctor: When you were in school — elementary, middle, high school, college — did teachers or professors ever give you feedback that you were careless, didn't pay attention to detail, or made silly mistakes on assignments or tests, even back then?\n\nPatient: Careless mistakes? No, see, that's the thing. Like I was saying about my film projects and how I've always been able to juggle a million things, the actual work was never the problem back then. I wasn't dropping the ball on the assignments or making silly errors on my tests. \n\nBut... if you're asking if teachers had feedback for me, oh, absolutely. They had plenty of feedback, it just wasn't about my attention to detail. It was... well, it was about my energy. Even way back in elementary school, I was just constantly getting talked to. I remember my teachers actually had to move my desk all the time. It was like this whole regular production where they'd move me right up next to the chalkboard, or isolate me in the back corner by the reading rug, because I was literally always out of my seat. I just couldn't sit still in those little plastic chairs, it felt like physical torture. \n\nI'd be up wandering around, or I'd just be blurting answers out loud before the teacher even finished asking the question. I just knew the answer and it would fly out of my mouth. And I was always bothering my classmates, like leaning over to talk to them or just being in their space, and the crazy thing is, I wouldn't even realize I was doing it until the teacher snapped at me to sit down and be quiet. It was just this physical need to move and engage with whatever was happening around me. \n\nSo my report cards were always full of comments about how I needed to practice self-control, or how I needed to learn to stay in my designated area, but never about my actual schoolwork being sloppy. I did fine on the tests! I just... I probably took them while standing up or bouncing my leg so hard the whole table shook. By the time I got to college, it was actually a lot better because I could just walk across campus between classes or throw myself into producing a massive, hands-on student film where I was running around set all day. The energy was an asset then. But sitting at a desk in grade school? Total disaster. But yeah, the careless mistakes thing? The missing the details? No, that really is just a completely recent thing tied to this new, agonizingly slow editing workflow I'm stuck in.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing those detailed technical tasks now — like labeling camera cards or reviewing timecodes — do you notice that you're fidgeting, feeling restless, or having trouble sitting still the way you used to in school? Or is it more that your mind just seems to wander or skip over the information even though your body feels calm?\n\nPatient: Calm? Oh, gosh, no. No, my body is definitely not calm. If anything, it's... it's almost exactly like it was in school, just, you know, manifested differently because I'm an adult in an office and not a kid on a reading rug. \n\nWhen I'm stuck doing those technical logs or staring at that massive spreadsheet trying to match up the timecodes, I am just constantly in motion. It's like this physical pressure builds up the longer I have to sit still and look at the tiny text. Like, especially in the edit reviews now, I’m constantly clicking a pen. Just over and over. I actually broke the spring in my favorite ballpoint last week because I was doing it so aggressively while trying to force myself to focus on this one incredibly tedious metadata field. \n\nAnd if I'm not clicking a pen, my knee is just going a mile a minute. I'm always bouncing my knee under the table, sometimes so hard I'm honestly surprised I haven't bruised my kneecap on the underside of the desk. And we have these fancy rolling chairs in the bays, which are supposed to be ergonomic, but for me, they're just... I don't know, an outlet for the restlessness? I am constantly rolling my chair back and forth, pushing off the edge of the desk, rolling back in, swiveling side to side while I wait for a file to upload or while I'm trying to read a note. \n\nIt’s actually become this whole running thing at the studio. People have started joking that they can hear me before they see me. They’ll be walking down the hall and hear the *click-click-click* of the pen and the squeak of the chair wheels and they know exactly where I am and what I'm doing. Because I literally cannot just sit static and look at the screen. The slower and more isolating the task is, the more my body just completely rebels and tries to create its own momentum. So, yeah, the physical restlessness is absolutely still there, it hasn't gone away at all, it's just channeled into office supplies and furniture now instead of wandering around a classroom.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on something that's genuinely engaging and interesting to you — like a complex multi-cam edit you mentioned earlier, or maybe color grading a really visually stunning sequence — do you find yourself making those same careless technical mistakes, or does your focus stay sharp on the details when the work itself is more stimulating?\n\nPatient: Right? The details don't slip at all. \n\nBut... and this is really the whole reason I'm here, honestly, what finally scared me into coming in to see a doctor... is that with all this new, tedious workflow stuff, I keep making these incredibly dumb technical mistakes that I absolutely know better than to make. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, back before the restructuring? No, I was completely sharp. I mean, my attention to detail was absolutely rock solid across the board. I was actually the go-to person for quality control on the really complex stuff because I just didn't miss things. But see, back then, my entire day was filled with those engaging projects. I was constantly stimulated. It was fast-paced, it was creative, it was everything I loved about the industry. I was thriving, honestly. 

But then the readjustment happened. So this whole situation, this really disruptive level of making these awful careless mistakes on the boring stuff, it’s been going on for about two years and two months now. Because that company readjustment completely changed how my shoots and edit reviews run. 

Like, I really have to paint a picture for you here, because the contrast is just night and day. Before, an edit review was this dynamic, collaborative thing. We'd be in the bay, throwing ideas around, cutting on the fly, experimenting with different color grades in real-time. It was electric. Now? Because of the new corporate protocols, an edit review is me sitting alone in a quiet room for four hours, filling out a standardized spreadsheet of timecodes and metadata tags for some producer who isn't even in the same state. 

And it's the same story with the shoots! Shoots used to be me running around with a rig on my shoulder, troubleshooting lighting setups, coordinating with the director, always moving. Now, because of how they restructured the departments two years and two months ago, they have me sitting at a video village monitor just logging clips and labeling those stupid camera cards while the junior guys get to do the actual operating. 

So, yeah, before that readjustment, I was golden because the environment and the tasks naturally kept me engaged. I never made those careless errors. But ever since they changed how the shoots and reviews operate and dumped all this static, tedious administrative work on my plate... that's exactly when my brain just started checking out and all these ridiculous, reputation-ruining mistakes started happening.

```json
{
  "intent": "Determine whether the patient's careless mistakes on tedious tasks represent a true deficit in attention to detail that exists independent of task engagement, or whether this is purely situational based on stimulus level. This will help clarify if we're dealing with ADHD (where inattention to detail persists across contexts but is worse with low-stimulation tasks) versus a stress response or adjustment disorder (where the mistakes are entirely contingent on the new work environment). I need to know if the patient ever made careless mistakes on boring tasks before the restructuring.",
  "question": "Before the company restructuring two years ago, when you had those slower, less stimulating moments—like routine file management or basic administrative tasks that weren't part of the exciting projects—did you ever make careless mistakes on that kind of work, or was your attention to detail sharp even on the boring stuff back then?"
```

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... if I really sit down and think about it, looking back to before all the corporate changes... yeah, I suppose I did. 

But see, the context was just so completely different back then. Before the restructuring, those slower, boring moments were incredibly rare. I usually had an assistant editor or a production assistant handling all the routine file management and the tedious paperwork. But on the off chance I *did* have to sit down and do the basic admin stuff? Yeah, my brain would still kind of just... check out. I might misplace a folder on the server, or I'd forget to log a lunch penalty on a timecard. But it was maybe one percent of my week! It was such a tiny fraction of my job that nobody really noticed, and I just brushed it off as being tired from a long shoot.

But now? Now that those boring tasks are basically my entire job description? It's a completely different story. And I know I must sound like a broken record here, I really do, but I have to stress this because it's exactly what finally scared me into coming in. I keep making these incredibly dumb technical mistakes that I absolutely know better than to make! It's exactly like I've been saying—labeling the camera cards wrong, or completely missing a timecode note on a spreadsheet, or that absolute nightmare of exporting a client cut with the wrong lower-third even after I've checked it. 

So, the inability to focus on the boring, routine stuff? That underlying issue was probably always there, just lurking in the background. But I used to have a fast-paced, creative role that naturally insulated me from it. Now I don't. Now I'm drowning in the tedious work, and those careless errors are happening constantly, and it's just... it's destroying my professional confidence.

  {
    "role": "system",

I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Dr. [Name]. Um, thank you for seeing me. So, I guess... well, I work in television and film production, mostly as a camera operator and a video editor. I've been doing it for years, ever since I got my bachelor's, and it's a really fast-paced environment, which I usually love, but lately... well, what finally scared me into actually making this appointment and coming in today is that I just keep making these really, really dumb technical mistakes. \n\nAnd I mean mistakes I absolutely know better than to make. It's not a lack of experience, it's just... I don't know, my brain just skips over things. Like, I'll be offloading footage after a twelve-hour shoot and I'll label the camera cards completely wrong, which is a massive headache for the whole post-production team. Or I'll be sitting in the edit bay and I'll just completely miss a specific timecode note the director gave me. \n\nBut the worst part, the part that really freaked me out and made me call your office, was when I exported a final client cut recently, and it had the completely wrong lower-third graphic on the screen with the CEO's name misspelled. And the crazy thing is, I checked it! I literally sat there, watched the timeline, told myself I was checking it for errors, and I still somehow just sent it off wrong. It's so incredibly embarrassing, and in this industry, your reputation is everything. If you're the editor who ruins a deadline because of a sloppy export, people stop hiring you. I just don't understand how I can look right at something, know exactly how it's supposed to be done, and still mess up the basic details like that. It's just making me so anxious about going into work every day.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks or months, have you noticed changes in your mood — like feeling persistently sad, empty, or down — separate from just the frustration about these work mistakes?\n\nPatient: No, no, not sad. I mean, not like that, anyway. I definitely get upset, and I might even cry out of pure frustration when I get home, but it's not like a dark cloud of sadness or feeling empty, you know? It's really just the anxiety and the stress from the mistakes. Like, just last Tuesday, I was driving home from the studio, and it was raining, and I just gripped the steering wheel so hard my knuckles were white, just replaying that lower-third graphic mistake over and over in my head. But that's not depression, I don't think. That's just me being incredibly mad at myself because I know I'm better than that. \n\nBecause when I'm not working, or when things actually go right—which they do, sometimes I nail a really complex multi-cam edit and it feels amazing—I feel totally fine! I have plenty of energy, I want to see my friends, I still love going out to the movies, which is huge for me since I work in film, you know? Some people get burned out and can't even look at a screen after a long week, but I still love it. I'm still me. \n\nIt's literally just this... this wall I hit when I have to focus on the tedious technical details, and then I mess them up, and *then* I feel bad. But it's a very specific \"I'm going to lose my job and ruin my reputation\" kind of bad, not a... not a sitting in the dark feeling empty kind of bad. Does that make sense? I just don't want you to think I'm depressed, because I really don't feel depressed, just... on edge. Constantly on edge about what stupid little detail I'm going to screw up next, and how much it's going to cost the production.\n\nDoctor: When you're not at work or thinking about work, do you notice that you have trouble concentrating or remembering things in your everyday life — like forgetting conversations, losing your keys, or having a hard time following a TV show or book — or is it mainly just at work when you're stressed?\n\nPatient: Well, um, it's funny you ask that, because I don't really think about it that much outside of work, but... no, I guess I don't really have trouble with that stuff? Like, if I'm watching a movie or a TV show, I'm completely locked in. I mean, obviously, I'm looking at the lighting and the cuts because of what I do for a living, but I never lose the plot or forget what's happening. I can sit through a three-hour director's cut of something and be totally absorbed the entire time. \n\nAnd as for conversations... no, I don't think I forget them. I'm actually the one who usually remembers random things my friends told me months ago. Like, my friend Sarah was talking about this very specific brand of vintage camera lens she wanted back in, I don't know, maybe October? And I remembered it just the other day when I saw one online and sent her the link. So my memory for things like that is fine. \n\nAnd losing things... I mean, everyone misplaces their keys once in a while, right? If I'm rushing out the door because I'm running late for a shoot, maybe I have to pat down my pockets or check the kitchen counter, but it's not like a constant, everyday struggle where I'm tearing the house apart looking for my wallet. My apartment is actually pretty organized, mostly because if it wasn't, I'd go crazy. I have specific hooks by the door for my keys and my work badge. So no, I wouldn't say I have a hard time with any of that in my normal life. It really just feels like it's the work stuff, the pressure and the technical details, that completely short-circuits my brain.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on routine, non-deadline tasks — like organizing files, doing basic maintenance on your equipment, or even just reviewing footage that doesn't have time pressure attached to it — do you find yourself making careless mistakes then too, or does it really only happen when you're under pressure or rushing?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that’s the thing. That is exactly what I was trying to explain earlier when I was talking about what finally scared me into coming in here. Because if it was just happening when we were on a tight turnaround, like a crazy same-day edit where the producers are literally standing over my shoulder, I would totally just chalk it up to stress. Everyone makes mistakes when they are rushing, right? \n\nBut... no. It happens even when I have all the time in the world. It happens during the most mundane, routine stuff. I can be just sitting in the ingest bay with my coffee on a quiet morning, nobody is breathing down my neck, the deadline isn't for another week. I'm just offloading footage, which should be the easiest thing in the world, and like I was saying before, I will still somehow label those camera cards completely wrong. \n\nAnd it’s the same with reviewing footage or organizing my project bins. I can be reviewing an interview without any time pressure at all, just casually watching it down to pull the best takes, and I will still completely miss a specific timecode note the director gave me. It's like my eyes are open, I'm looking right at the screen, but my brain just decides not to process the numbers. \n\nEven that final client cut I told you about, the one with the wrong lower-third graphic and the misspelled CEO name? I actually had a full forty-eight hours to deliver that. I wasn't rushing at all! I literally sat there at my desk, leaned back in my chair, and told myself that I was going to do a slow, careful check for errors. I watched the whole timeline. I checked it, I really did, and I still exported it wrong and sent it off. \n\nSo, yeah, it's definitely not just the pressure or the rushing. The pressure makes the consequences worse, obviously, but the actual mistakes, those really dumb technical mistakes that I absolutely know better than to make, they happen even when I'm just doing basic file management or routine work. And honestly, I think that's what's so terrifying about it to me. If I can't even get the basic, low-stakes stuff right when I'm totally relaxed and have plenty of time, how am I supposed to trust myself at all? It just makes me feel like I'm losing my edge completely.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these careless mistakes and trouble with focus on technical details? Was this something that's been happening your whole life, or is this a relatively new development for you in the past few months or years?\n\nPatient: My whole life? Oh, gosh, no. No, definitely not my whole life. I mean, I’ve always been a very high-energy person, you know, always moving a million miles a minute, which is actually great when you're on a busy set, but the actual mistakes? The dropping the ball on the technical details and just totally missing things right in front of my face? That is definitely newer. \n\nIf I really sit down and trace it back to when it started getting bad... like, really disruptive to my job... it’s been going on for about two years and two months. Yeah, exactly two years and two months, because it was right at the start of the year, two years ago, when our studio went through this massive company readjustment. \n\nSee, before that readjustment, things were structured a lot differently. I had a totally different rhythm. But then management came in, they restructured the departments to cut costs, and they completely changed how my shoots and edit reviews run. Like, completely overhauled the entire pipeline. It used to be that we had a very collaborative, in-person review process—we'd all sit in a bay, the producers, the director, and we'd watch the cut together and talk through it. I have to upload my cuts to this web portal, fill out all these tedious metadata fields, type up my own technical logs, and then just wait for timecode notes to be emailed back to me in this massive spreadsheet. \n\nIt completely shifted the burden of all that tiny, granular technical stuff onto my plate in a way it wasn't before, and stripped away the parts of the process that kept me engaged. I kept telling myself, \"Oh, you're just adjusting to the new workflow, it'll click eventually, everyone takes time to learn a new system.\" But it hasn't clicked! So, yeah, it's not a lifelong thing at all, it's very specifically tied to when they changed how we work. I just... Was this something that's been happening your whole life, or is this a relatively new development for you in the past few months or years?\n\nPatient: My whole life? Oh, gosh, no. No, definitely not my whole life. I mean, I’ve always been a very high-energy person, you know, always moving a million miles a minute, which is actually great when you're on a busy set, but the actual mistakes? The dropping the ball on the technical details and just totally missing things right in front of my face? That is definitely newer. \n\nIf I really sit down and trace it back to when it started getting bad... like, really disruptive to my job... it’s been going on for about two years and two months. Yeah, exactly two years and two months, because it was right at the start of the year, two years ago, when our studio went through this massive company readjustment. \n\nSee, before that readjustment, things were structured a lot differently. I had a totally different rhythm. But then management came in, they restructured the departments to cut costs, and they completely changed how my shoots and edit reviews run. Like, completely overhauled the entire pipeline. It used to be that we had a very collaborative, in-person review process—we'd all sit in a bay, the producers, the director, and we'd watch the cut together and talk through it. It was very engaging, very dynamic. I thrived in that environment. \n\nBut the readjustment changed all of that. Now, the shoots are scheduled differently, so I'm bouncing between projects way more without a lot of transition time, and the edit reviews are almost entirely remote and asynchronous. I have to upload my cuts to this web portal, fill out all these tedious metadata fields, type up my own technical logs, and then just wait for timecode notes to be emailed back to me in this massive spreadsheet. \n\nIt completely shifted the burden of all that tiny, granular technical stuff onto my plate in a way it wasn't before, and stripped away the parts of the process that kept me engaged. And that is exactly when this started. Two years and two months of just... constantly missing things in the new system. I kept telling myself, \"Oh, you're just adjusting to the new workflow, it'll click eventually, everyone takes time to learn a new system.\" But it hasn't clicked! It's been over two years, and I'm still making the same stupid mistakes on the reviews that I was making the week they rolled the changes out. So, yeah, it's not a lifelong thing at all, it's very specifically tied to when they changed how we work. I just... I don't know why I can't adapt to it like everyone else seems to have.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you've always been a high-energy person, moving a mile a minute. Before the company restructuring two years ago, did you ever struggle with staying organized, keeping track of details, or managing multiple tasks at once — even if it wasn't as noticeable in your old work environment?\n\nPatient: Honestly? No. No, not at all. I mean, if anything, I was actually known for being the one who could juggle a million things at once. Because when you're a camera operator and an editor—which I do both, you know, depending on the project, and that’s pretty common in the commercial space—you absolutely have to be organized. And I was! Before they changed everything, I was the person everyone relied on to keep all the plates spinning. \n\nI could be on a busy set, managing three different camera setups, keeping track of exactly which lenses we used for which specific shots so the continuity matched perfectly in post, and I’d be talking to the director about the next setup while simultaneously packing away the fragile gear from the last one. Multitasking was just... it was my bread and butter. It never felt like a struggle. Even going way back to when I was getting my bachelor's degree in film, I was always the one producing the big senior thesis projects because I could handle having my hands in everything. The details never slipped through the cracks back then because the environment was just so dynamic and alive. \n\nAnd it was the exact same in the edit bay. I had my project bins organized flawlessly—audio here, b-roll there, interviews color-coded by subject. And it wasn't something I had to force myself to do or white-knuckle my way through, it was just how I worked. The fast pace actually made me more organized, if that makes sense? Like, the momentum of moving a mile a minute kept me sharp. I thrived on that kind of controlled chaos, honestly. \n\nSo, no, I didn't struggle with keeping track of details or managing multiple tasks back then, not even subtly. It never felt like a heavy lift, and I never dropped the ball on the important stuff. I had my own rhythm, my own system, and it worked perfectly for me for years and years. I mean, I wouldn't have gotten to the level I'm at in my career if I couldn't handle the details, right? You just can't survive in television or film if you're inherently disorganized. I was doing great. It really is just this new, slow, isolated, spreadsheet-heavy workflow that completely paralyzes me. But before that? I was completely on top of it.\n\nDoctor: When you were in school — elementary, middle, high school, college — did teachers or professors ever give you feedback that you were careless, didn't pay attention to detail, or made silly mistakes on assignments or tests, even back then?\n\nPatient: Careless mistakes? No, see, that's the thing. Like I was saying about my film projects and how I've always been able to juggle a million things, the actual work was never the problem back then. I wasn't dropping the ball on the assignments or making silly errors on my tests. \n\nBut... if you're asking if teachers had feedback for me, oh, absolutely. They had plenty of feedback, it just wasn't about my attention to detail. It was... well, it was about my energy. Even way back in elementary school, I was just constantly getting talked to. I remember my teachers actually had to move my desk all the time. It was like this whole regular production where they'd move me right up next to the chalkboard, or isolate me in the back corner by the reading rug, because I was literally always out of my seat. I just couldn't sit still in those little plastic chairs, it felt like physical torture. \n\nI'd be up wandering around, or I'd just be blurting answers out loud before the teacher even finished asking the question. I just knew the answer and it would fly out of my mouth. And I was always bothering my classmates, like leaning over to talk to them or just being in their space, and the crazy thing is, I wouldn't even realize I was doing it until the teacher snapped at me to sit down and be quiet. It was just this physical need to move and engage with whatever was happening around me. \n\nSo my report cards were always full of comments about how I needed to practice self-control, or how I needed to learn to stay in my designated area, but never about my actual schoolwork being sloppy. I did fine on the tests! I just... I probably took them while standing up or bouncing my leg so hard the whole table shook. By the time I got to college, it was actually a lot better because I could just walk across campus between classes or throw myself into producing a massive, hands-on student film where I was running around set all day. The energy was an asset then. But sitting at a desk in grade school? Total disaster. But yeah, the careless mistakes thing? The missing the details? No, that really is just a completely recent thing tied to this new, agonizingly slow editing workflow I'm stuck in.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing those detailed technical tasks now — like labeling camera cards or reviewing timecodes — do you notice that you're fidgeting, feeling restless, or having trouble sitting still the way you used to in school? Or is it more that your mind just seems to wander or skip over the information even though your body feels calm?\n\nPatient: Calm? Oh, gosh, no. No, my body is definitely not calm. If anything, it's... it's almost exactly like it was in school, just, you know, manifested differently because I'm an adult in an office and not a kid on a reading rug. \n\nWhen I'm stuck doing those technical logs or staring at that massive spreadsheet trying to match up the timecodes, I am just constantly in motion. I actually broke the spring in my favorite ballpoint last week because I was doing it so aggressively while trying to force myself to focus on this one incredibly tedious metadata field. \n\nAnd if I'm not clicking a pen, my knee is just going a mile a minute. I'm always bouncing my knee under the table, sometimes so hard I'm honestly surprised I haven't bruised my kneecap on the underside of the desk. And we have these fancy rolling chairs in the bays, which are supposed to be ergonomic, but for me, they're just... I don't know, an outlet for the restlessness? I am constantly rolling my chair back and forth, pushing off the edge of the desk, rolling back in, swiveling side to side while I wait for a file to upload or while I'm trying to read a note. \n\nIt’s actually become this whole running thing at the studio. People have started joking that they can hear me before they see me. They’ll be walking down the hall and hear the *click-click-click* of the pen and the squeak of the chair wheels and they know exactly where I am and what I'm doing. See, that's exactly the distinction, and it's so unbelievably frustrating. I'm adjusting the shadows, pushing the midtones, making sure the skin tones match perfectly across three different camera sensors—my focus is absolute. I am completely locked in. I don't miss a single pixel. Or if I'm doing a fast-paced multi-cam edit where I'm cutting right on the beat of the music, I'm incredibly sharp. The details don't slip at all. \n\nBut... and this is really the whole reason I'm here, honestly, what finally scared me into coming in to see a doctor... is that with all this new, tedious workflow stuff, I keep making these incredibly dumb technical mistakes that I absolutely know better than to make. It's like my brain just completely shuts off if the task isn't stimulating enough. \n\nLike, I'll be labeling camera cards, which is the most basic, Day-One intern level stuff in the world. Or I'll be going through the producer's spreadsheet and I will completely miss a timecode note. The client had to flag it, and I was just... I was mortified. I mean, my reputation is everything in this industry, you know? You live and die by your reliability.
